Successful category definitions (REST constraints, twelve-factor, local-first's seven ideals) are short and implementation-neutral. Split the list: 6-8 neutral category criteria (identity-bound actors, scoped authority, receipts, revocation, settlement-awareness, declared admission), and move the remaining 18+ into an 'IOI conformance profile' section or into their owner docs. Otherwise the category claim reads as a moat dressed as a standard.
9. **web4-and-ioi-stack.md carries UI-shell-altitude detail in the category doc, creating a proven drift engine** — `medium` · Refactor
   - Evidence: foundations/web4-and-ioi-stack.md:267-269 — 'Shell-Placed Owner Application / Automations / one owner-application registration with a permanent launch placement'
   - Recommendation: The category/stack doctrine enumerates Core Workspaces, the Automations shell placement, per-surface application lists, and the 'conditional Embodied Systems owner_application planned registration' twice (lines 263-280 and 385-388), duplicating core-clients-surfaces.md at a wildly lower altitude than 'what is Web4.' Every taxonomy PR must re-edit the category definition — verified: this file sits in the modified set of the current taxonomy-convergence wave. Also the 'IOI Reference Stack' (219-294) and 'IOI System Boundary' (368-411) are two overlapping stack lists with divergent names (Mixture of Workers at :256 vs MoW Routing at :389; Hypervisor Daemon / Runtime Nodes at :253 vs Execution Nodes at :381). Keep one protocol-altitude stack diagram; link to surface/workspace owners for everything below Hypervisor Core.
10. **Reputation and dispute packets sit in the core vocabulary while the doc admits no model exists — inviting the exact fragmentation Non-Negotiable 4 forbids** — `medium` · Red flag
   - Evidence: foundations/aiip.md:483-484 — 'ReputationQueryPacket / Return reputation under this context, rubric, worker class, or dispute history.'
   - Recommendation: Open workstreams concede 'context-aware reputation models' and 'dispute models and evidence disclosure rules' are undefined (aiip.md:973-974), yet ReputationQueryPacket, DisputePacket, and DisputeResolutionPacket are core message types. Portable cross-domain reputation is a decades-unsolved problem (EigenTrust through draft ERC-8004); shipping the packet before the semantics guarantees each bilateral pair invents incompatible meanings — precisely 'isolated authority, reputation, and receipt standards' (aiip.md:933-934). Demote reputation to an extension profile whose core-carried object is only an opaque signed evidence ref; keep disputes in core but only as a typed escalation hook referencing the channel's declared dispute_policy_ref, with resolution semantics living in the settlement profile.
11. **Canonical serialization, signature coverage, and hash agility are undecided and missing from the workstream list** — `medium` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: foundations/aiip.md:969 — 'The canonical doctrine is settled, but protocol specs still need to define:'
   - Recommendation: The envelopes are YAML sketches with 'hash' and 'signature: base64' fields; coverage rules exist only piecemeal (AIIPEnvelope.payload_hash 'binds the complete payload' and, for collaboration-terms responses, response_hash 'binds every response field except the signature,' aiip.md:525-526) with no canonical byte encoding, envelope-wide signature-coverage rule, or algorithm-agility policy. Cross-organization signed messages historically fail on canonicalization, not cryptography (XML-DSig/SAML wrapping attacks), which is why modern protocols pick deterministic CBOR/COSE or JCS+JWS up front. The Open Protocol Workstreams list (aiip.md:969-997) does not include serialization, canonical hashing, signature-coverage rules, or hash/signature agility. Add a workstream: one canonical byte encoding per schema_version, explicit sign-what-you-hash coverage (envelope minus signature block), and an algorithm-agility policy consistent with the ml-dsa/hybrid ambition already in the signature enum.
12. **'Blockchain-native underneath' contradicts AIIP's no-consensus assumption and the pack's consensus-neutrality requirement** — `medium` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: foundations/web4-and-ioi-stack.md:120-121 — 'Web4 is blockchain-native underneath, but it must not be chain-first in ordinary product experience.' vs foundations/aiip.md:22 — 'AIIP assumes neither consensus nor IOI L1.'
   - Recommendation: web4's own requirement 25 (web4-and-ioi-stack.md:361-363) declares no-consensus/PoA-1 a first-class profile, aiip.md makes L1 strictly optional, invariants.md itself insists a commitment chain 'is not relabeled a blockchain; consensus and tokens remain optional,' and the default deployment (single Hypervisor node, local receipts, local_domain settlement) contains no blockchain at all — so 'blockchain-native underneath' is false for the canonical default path and hands skeptics an easy hit. Reword the section to what the architecture actually guarantees: 'verifiability-native underneath' — signed receipts, roots, and commitments with optional chain anchoring under explicit enrollment. Reserve 'blockchain' for the IOI L1 and external-chain settlement profiles where it is literally true.
13. **Peer identity fields accept two URI schemes (system:// | domain://) with no rule for which one names an AIIP party** — `medium` · Rename
   - Evidence: foundations/common-objects-and-envelopes.md:2455 — 'system_id_from: system://... | domain://...'
   - Recommendation: INV-32 defines the sovereignty boundary as the system_id, yet every channel/envelope from/to field also admits domain://, and BoundedExecutionDomainEnvelope keys on domain_id: domain:// — so the same counterparty can appear under either scheme, muddying the exact boundary the doctrine labors to draw (a domain inside a system_id must not be an independent AIIP peer). Pick one: AIIP peers are always system:// (the independently governed boundary), and domain:// is reserved for bounded-execution-domain descriptors within or beneath a system. Rename fields to party_system_id_from/to and drop the alternate scheme; add a vocabulary.md entry disambiguating IOI's multiple overloaded uses of 'domain' (bounded execution domain, Domain Ontology, Agentgres/application domain, domain kernel, sovereign domain) — verified: no such disambiguation entry exists in vocabulary.md today.
14. **Relayer economics is a non-negotiable but no relay/router actor exists anywhere in the object model** — `medium` · Red flag
   - Evidence: foundations/aiip.md:935-936 — 'Do not depend on unpaid relayers as public-good infrastructure for consequential handoffs.'
   - Recommendation: The doc correctly imports IBC's hardest operational lesson, but the architecture defines no relay actor: AIIPChannelEnvelope has relay_policy_ref: optional and transport values https|queue|chain_relay (common-objects-and-envelopes.md:2426-2429), and 'relay/router fee, bond, SLA, and penalty mechanics' is explicitly deferred (aiip.md:977). Who moves packets between two Hypervisors behind NAT/firewalls, and who pays them, is undefined — a promise without a mechanism. Either scope v1 conformance explicitly to direct bilateral HTTPS (no third-party relayers; the non-negotiable then applies to future federated profiles), or define a minimal RelayProfileEnvelope (identity, fee basis, delivery receipt obligation, bond ref) now so channel establishment can bind it.
15. **The acronym AIIP does not decode to its own title expansion** — `low` · Rename
   - Evidence: foundations/aiip.md:1 — '# AIIP: Inter-Autonomous-System Protocol'
   - Recommendation: 'Inter-Autonomous-System Protocol' yields IASP, not AIIP; no file under docs/architecture ever expands AIIP letter-for-letter (verified by search), and the thesis language ('bounded autonomous institutions,' aiip.md:34-35) suggests the intended expansion involves institutions or interop. For a protocol whose adoption pitch targets external implementers, an acronym that cannot be decoded from its canonical title reads as carelessness in the first line of the spec. Fix once in the owner doc: either retitle to match the letters — e.g. 'Autonomous Institution Interoperation Protocol (AIIP)' — or keep the current descriptive title and state 'AIIP (Autonomous-system Inter-Institution Protocol)' explicitly, then propagate through vocabulary.md.

### IOI L1 mainnet + contract interfaces

**Files reviewed:** `foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md (485)` · `foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md (447)`

**Current shape.** Two canonical foundations docs define IOI L1 as an optional, opt-in public trust layer for an ecosystem of sovereign L0 autonomous systems: it owns ai:// namespace registration, publisher/domain/system identity and manifest commitments, rights/license/escrow/SLA-bond/dispute contracts, reputation/contribution/receipt root anchoring, AIIP channel/schema registries, a shared-security service market (validators, verifiers, guardians, availability witnesses, arbitrators posting bonds under explicit terms), and protocol governance. Systems relate to it through a three-profile enrollment envelope — ioi_compatible (no L1 obligation at all), ioi_connected (pays only for named services), ioi_secured (additionally adopts a Standard DAS conformance profile and named shared-security coverage) — with fail-closed, compare-and-swap, authority-proven mutations and exit that preserves obligations. A strict gas boundary keeps all operational volume off-chain: "the chain prices commitments, the runtime prices work" — model calls, tool calls, receipts, GoalRuns, OutcomeRoom transitions, and Agentgres writes never touch L1. The interfaces file sketches 33 Solidity contracts, from hardened ones (AutonomousSystemRegistry, NetworkEnrollmentRegistry, ServiceOrderEscrow with predecessor-root CAS and authority/adjudication proofs) to bare root registries and bond/slash functions. A bootstrap gate says no sovereign chain or native asset until real contract demand is proven on a devnet or mature existing settlement environment; any eventual native asset is risk-bearing capital, gas, and bond collateral — explicitly not Work Credits, inference currency, or a per-system token. First-party apps aiagent.xyz and sas.xyz are positioned as the seed protocol clients whose rich state stays in their own Agentgres domains. Both files are marked Implementation status: speculative — pure design authority for a future chain.

**What it already gets right:**

- Demand-gated launch discipline is rare and correct: 'Contract and service demand should be proven before a sovereign mainnet or native asset' (ioi-l1-mainnet.md:465) plus the explicit refusal to predict market capitalization (lines 422-423) inverts the typical token-first L1 whitepaper failure mode.
- The gas boundary is unusually concrete and honest — two enumerated lists (mainnet 268-316) that keep per-thought/per-tool/per-receipt volume off-chain, exactly the lesson Helium and Celo learned the hard way; and line 97 concedes 'L0 or AIIP adoption alone does not create L1 transactions, fees, bonded demand, or token value,' which almost no token doc admits.
- Enrollment is a real fail-closed state machine (propose/activate/suspend/exit with obligation preservation, CAS on predecessor roots, interfaces 144-161), and 'ioi_secured is not a blanket claim that a system is safe' (mainnet 111-112) is anti-marketing assurance honesty ahead of most shared-security offerings.
- The hardened contracts (AutonomousSystemRegistry, NetworkEnrollmentRegistry, ServiceOrderEscrow) show a genuine security methodology — expected-prior-root CAS, authority proofs, staged assurance consumption ('the escrow consumes admitted delivery, acceptance, adjudication... roots; it does not author those assurance stages', interfaces 260-261) — consistent with the pack's fail-closed physics.
- Positioning first-party apps (aiagent.xyz, sas.xyz) as seed demand generators (mainnet 320-325) is the right answer to the registry cold-start problem: the protocol does not depend on third parties showing up first.

**SOTA verdict: `behind`** — compared against: Ethereum rollup-centric roadmap + L2Beat stages framework (custody, upgradeability, exit guarantees); EigenLayer AVS / Cosmos Interchain Security / Symbiotic (shared-security marketplaces with operator sets, slashing veto committees); ENS (commit-reveal registration, non-seizable namespace governance); UMA optimistic oracle / Kleros / Arbitrum BoLD (challenge-resolution game semantics); Celestia / EigenDA / Ethereum blobs (data-availability as the crux of off-chain-state, on-chain-commitment designs); Helium migration to Solana (2023) and Celo L1-to-L2 migration (sovereign-L1 abandonment base rate); Helium/POKT burn-and-mint equilibrium literature (BME fits continuous metered usage, not sparse lumpy commitments); MiCA CASP / SEC posture on fee-plus-governance native assets and custodial escrow

**Where the state of the art is ahead (gaps):**

- No security-budget model: nothing connects sparse-by-design fee/bond volume to the cost of running and securing a sovereign validator set; SOTA appchain analyses quantify this before launch
- Challenge functions (reputation, receipt, handoff, eligibility roots) have no resolution game — no bond, window, escalation, or timeout default, unlike UMA/Kleros/BoLD
- No data/evidence-availability binding: challenges presume private Agentgres evidence stays available from a possibly hostile or dead counterparty; exit-time 'evidence-retention duties' are named in prose but no retention bond or witness linkage exists per committed root
- No custody doctrine for funds in escrow/bonds — no timelocks, escape hatches, or forced-exit path on contract upgrade (L2Beat stages territory), and no bridge posture for stable assets on a sovereign chain
- No anti-front-running or anti-squatting mechanics for ai:// names (ENS solved this with commit-reveal a cycle ago)
- No fee-denomination doctrine despite payable sketches; BME appropriateness never analyzed even though start-here routes 'token/BME' questions here (the flywheel doc defers token/BME timing but analyzes no mechanism fit)
- Shared-security market reinvents EigenLayer/ICS without operator-set, stake-denomination, or cross-service slashing-contention mechanics, and without the option to rent existing restaking security

**Findings (15, severity-ordered):**

1. **Security-budget circularity of a sparse-commitment sovereign chain is never modeled** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:412 — 'A sovereign IOI L1 becomes justified when neutral ordering/security, sovereignty, performance, or governance creates more value than the migration and security burden.'
   - Recommendation: The chain is sparse by design, so fee volume is structurally low; validators must then be paid from inflation of an asset whose value depends on those same sparse fees — a death spiral the existing Bootstrap And Native-Asset Gate (mainnet:407-423) acknowledges only qualitatively ('migration and security burden') and never closes. Add a 'Security Budget Doctrine' section to ioi-l1-mainnet.md: (a) define the launch threshold quantitatively (annualized fee + bonded-demand vs. cost-of-corruption of the validator set); (b) invert the default — canonize 'deploy contracts on an existing settlement environment' as the durable end-state and sovereign L1 as a contingency ADR, citing Helium-to-Solana and Celo-to-L2 as the base rate. Today the sovereign-vs-external decision itself gets roughly fifteen qualitative lines, and nothing in either file models the security economics that decision hinges on.
2. **The two canonical owners enumerate divergent contract sets after the same alignment pass** — `high` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:199 — '- `VerificationProfileRegistry`' (absent from interfaces); ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:54-55 list RuntimeProviderRegistry/AttestationProfileRegistry (absent from mainnet families)
   - Recommendation: Both files claim canonical ownership and both carry 'Last alignment pass: 2026-07-12,' yet: VerificationProfileRegistry exists only in mainnet; AttestationProfileRegistry and RuntimeProviderRegistry only in interfaces (whose gas list even bills 'runtime provider registration/bonding' that mainnet's gas list omits); mainnet's ten sas.xyz contracts (ServiceOrder, ServiceEscrow, DeliveryAcceptance, ProviderPayout, SLASlash...) are silently consolidated into ServiceOrderEscrow/SLABondRegistry/DisputeRegistry in interfaces; TreasuryGrant and ValidatorOrGuardianSetUpdate (mainnet:259-260) have no interface at all. Fix by making ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md the single owner of the enumerated contract set; mainnet keeps duty families with zero contract names; add a name-parity check to the architecture-docs gate.
3. **Value-bearing slash/revoke/accept functions lack the CAS + adjudication + authority hardening the flagship contracts got** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:270 — 'function slashBond(bytes32 providerId, bytes32 serviceId, uint256 amount, string reason) external;'
   - Recommendation: AutonomousSystemRegistry, NetworkEnrollmentRegistry, and ServiceOrderEscrow bind expected-prior roots, adjudication roots, and authority proofs; but slashBond takes a free string reason, revokeInstallRight(licenseId, reason) lets an unspecified caller destroy a paid right, acceptSettlementIntent(intentId) carries no acceptor authority, and commitReputationRoot/commitReceiptRoot/commitAuthorityLease have no enrollment or CAS binding — inconsistent with the fail-closed hardening the same file mandates for its flagship contracts ('Missing, stale, suspended, or mismatched inputs fail closed', interfaces:131-132) and with mainnet's rule that a stale or unauthorized mutation cannot authorize a later registry or settlement operation. ServiceBondRegistry.resolveBondClaim already binds an adjudicationRoot, proving the pattern generalizes. Apply the SystemMutationProof pattern uniformly: every mutating function on SLABondRegistry, LicenseRightRegistry, SettlementIntentRegistry, ReputationRootRegistry, ReceiptRootRegistry, AuthorityLeaseCommitmentRegistry, HandoffFinalityRegistry takes expectedPrior root, adjudication/decision root, and authorityProofRoot.
4. **Challenge functions have no dispute-game semantics — they emit and stop** — `high` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:291 — 'function challengeReputationRoot(bytes32 domainId, uint256 epoch, bytes32 evidenceRoot) external;'
   - Recommendation: challengeReputationRoot, challengeReceiptRoot, challengeHandoff, challengeWorkerEligibility, and challengeSettlementIntent define no challenge bond, no response window, no escalation path, no timeout default, and no effect on the challenged root's status. A staged DisputeRegistry pipeline exists (open/evidence/decision/appeal/finalize, interfaces:274-285) and challengeSettlementIntent even carries a disputeRoot, but nothing binds the other challenge functions to it or defines the game. SOTA (UMA's optimistic oracle, Kleros, Arbitrum BoLD) treats a challenge as the opening move of a defined game. Define one shared OptimisticChallenge primitive in the interfaces file: challenger bond, respondent liveness window, escalation into DisputeRegistry, root suspension or confirmation on timeout, and bond distribution on resolution — then have every challenge function reference it instead of being a bare event emitter.
5. **No evidence-availability binding: disputes presume private Agentgres data survives a hostile counterparty** — `high` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:439 — 'Every contract root must be resolvable to Agentgres refs and storage-backend evidence when required.'
   - Recommendation: Agentgres is local-first and private; if the committing system exits, dies, or turns adversarial, the evidence behind its committed roots vanishes and every challenge/dispute against those roots becomes unadjudicable. The pack gestures at this — enrollment exit 'preserves outstanding obligations, disputes, evidence-retention duties, and final commitments' (mainnet:119-121) and an 'availability-witness' service kind exists (mainnet:51) — but no mechanism binds a specific committed root to a retention obligation, and nothing covers a live but uncooperative counterparty. Add an EvidenceRetentionBinding to root-committing calls: root class → named availability-witness agreement or retention bond, with claimable slashing if evidence is unavailable during a live challenge window. This is the crux insight of Celestia/EigenDA/blob designs: off-chain state with on-chain commitments is only as trustworthy as its data availability.
6. **Escrow and bonds custody real funds with no upgradeability, exit, or bridge doctrine** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:253 — 'function createOrder(bytes32 serviceId, address customer, address provider, bytes32 orderRoot, uint256 amount, address token) external payable...'
   - Recommendation: ServiceOrderEscrow, ServiceBondRegistry, and SLABondRegistry hold user money from day one, yet neither file states whether contracts are immutable, proxy-upgradeable under ProtocolGovernance (a rug vector), or timelocked with user escape hatches — and mainnet:394 explicitly lists 'settlement contract upgrades' as a governance duty with no custody safeguard, which sharpens rather than answers the question. The L2Beat stages framework is the industry bar here. And `address token` on a sovereign chain implies bridged stablecoins, historically the single largest loss vector (Ronin, Wormhole). Add custody non-negotiables: escrow/bond contracts are immutable or timelock-upgraded with a forced-exit path that survives malicious governance; plus a settlement-asset doctrine naming admissible assets and the bridge/custody posture per deployment substrate.
7. **ai:// namespace has an unspecified seizure power and front-runnable registration** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:65 — 'function revokeNamespace(string aiName, string reason) external;'
   - Recommendation: A 'neutral public-trust utility' whose root namespace can be revoked by an unspecified caller on a string reason is not neutral — this is the exact credibility failure ENS governance was designed to avoid. And registerNamespace(string aiName,...) is mempool-front-runnable with no commit-reveal and no squatting policy (neither concept appears anywhere in the pack). Fix: (a) commit-reveal two-step registration; (b) revocation only via a DisputeRegistry adjudicationRoot parameter, never operator fiat; (c) a one-paragraph name-policy doctrine (premium names, auctions or rate limits) in ioi-l1-mainnet.md's AiNamespaceRegistry duty.
8. **Interfaces non-negotiable 4 presumes a base L1 that mainnet non-negotiable 7 says is not dogma** — `medium` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:441 — 'L2/rollup is optional scaling, not base architecture.' vs ioi-l1-mainnet.md:466 — 'L2s/rollups and external settlement are options, not dogma.'
   - Recommendation: The interfaces file frames L2/rollup as scaling OF an assumed base chain; the mainnet file keeps external settlement (including deploying onto an existing L2) open as a first-class terminal option — under which the L2 IS the base architecture. The interfaces Purpose (line 14) does call IOI L1 'optional', so the file is not wholly committed, but NN4's phrasing still tells an implementer reading only this file that a sovereign base chain is settled doctrine. Rewrite interfaces NN4 to match the mainnet posture: 'These interfaces are deployment-substrate-neutral: a mature external chain, an L2/rollup, or a sovereign IOI chain are all admissible hosts; substrate choice follows the Bootstrap And Native-Asset Gate.'
9. **State the fee-denomination doctrine now; burn-and-mint is a structural misfit for sparse lumpy demand** — `medium` · Better way
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:416 — 'Any eventual native asset is conditional risk-bearing capital and scarce public-capacity access.'
   - Recommendation: start-here.md:388 routes 'token/BME' questions to this file, and economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md ('IOI L1, Token, and BME', lines 869-899) already defers token/BME design until verified demand and lists the native asset's defensible roles — but no doc anywhere analyzes whether BME structurally fits, and neither L1 doc states a fee-denomination doctrine despite payable sketches. Do the analysis once, canonically: BME (Helium/POKT) stabilizes continuous metered service usage; IOI L1 demand is sparse and lumpy (registrations, escrows, disputes), where BME adds reflexivity without smoothing anything — thin burn volume makes token price hypersensitive to single large commitments. Canonize as conditional doctrine: fees are stable-denominated (whatever the substrate), the native asset's only roles are validator security, service bonds, and governance, and BME is explicitly rejected unless continuous metered network service demand later emerges. This closes the gap the flywheel doc's defer-only posture leaves open.
10. **'Asset-neutral' non-negotiable vs payable-native fee sketches throughout the interfaces** — `medium` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:446 — 'Contract sketches remain asset-neutral until real service demand and native-asset governance are separately approved.' vs :62 'external payable'
   - Recommendation: Solidity `payable` means the host chain's native asset — so registerNamespace, registerPublisher, registerSystem, proposeEnrollment, openDispute, etc. are already native-asset-denominated in the sketch, contradicting NN7's asset-neutrality claim. The file already uses the right pattern elsewhere: postServiceBond takes `address asset` (:188) and createOrder takes `address token` (:253). Extend it — replace `payable` across the sketches with explicit (address feeAsset, uint256 feeAmount) parameters or a FeePolicyRegistry indirection resolved per deployment substrate. This is not copyediting: the payable pattern silently commits the design to native-gas fee capture, which is exactly the decision the Bootstrap And Native-Asset Gate defers.
11. **Shared-security market should rent existing restaking/ICS security, not only bootstrap a bespoke bonded market** — `medium` · Better way *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:127 — 'Their security comes from named responsibility and capital at risk, not from the label "decentralized."'
   - Recommendation: The Shared-Security Service Market is EigenLayer-AVS/Cosmos-ICS territory without their hard-won mechanics: operator-set sizing, stake denomination, cross-service slashing contention, and a slashing veto/appeal committee for buggy slash conditions. Mainnet:130-131 already allows a system to 'use external security while remaining compatible', but only on the consumer side. Two concrete changes: (1) state that SharedSecurityAgreement providers MAY be external restaking/ICS operator sets fulfilling the same terms/bond/claims envelope — this imports mature bonded capital instead of bootstrapping it and further weakens the case for a sovereign chain; (2) add a SlashingReviewCommittee object between openBondClaim and resolveBondClaim (which today binds only a single adjudicationRoot), mirroring EigenLayer's veto committee, so a defective claim policy cannot destroy honest provider capital.
12. **On-chain payouts are not bound to the pack's own jurisdiction/eligibility policy machinery** — `medium` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-contract-interfaces.md:256 — 'function releasePayout(uint256 orderId, bytes32 expectedPriorOrderStateRoot, bytes32 acceptanceDecisionRoot, bytes32 settlementIntentRoot, bytes32 authorityProofRoot) external;'
   - Recommendation: ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md defines kyc_required/sanctions_screening_required policy packs (lines 267-269) and wallet.network owns KYC factors — but nothing in the escrow flow requires payouts to resolve through a SettlementAccountRegistry account whose policyRoot carries those eligibility refs (createOrder takes raw customer/provider addresses), so a sanctioned or ineligible provider can receive irreversible on-chain funds. Require releasePayout/applyAdjudicatedRemedy to pay only registered settlement accounts and bind their policyRoot at lock time. Also add one honest paragraph on the operator posture: escrow + arbitration + a fee-and-governance native asset is MiCA-CASP/money-transmission territory, and the docs currently name no accountable operating entity for it.
13. **'IOI L1' as a name front-runs the very decision the bootstrap gate defers** — `medium` · Rename *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:409 — 'The architecture does not require a sovereign L1 at product launch.'
   - Recommendation: The doctrine says the sovereign chain may never launch and the contracts may live permanently on an external settlement environment — yet every object, file, and vocabulary entry is branded 'IOI L1,' which signals to partners, regulators, and token speculators that a chain-plus-token is the committed destination. Rename the layer 'IOI Settlement Layer' or 'IOI Public Trust Layer' (files: ioi-settlement-layer.md, settlement-contract-interfaces.md), keeping 'IOI L1' only inside the Bootstrap And Native-Asset Gate as the named contingency. The pack already retired 'AppDomainRegistry' for DomainRegistry (interfaces:87-89), so the rename convention exists; note the term is referenced pack-wide (vocabulary, start-here, source-of-truth-map, invariants INV-10/11, whitepaper), so this must be one deliberate coordinated pass.
14. **'Standard DAS' collides with Data Availability Sampling for exactly this spec's audience** — `medium` · Rename
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:108 — 'Additionally adopts an approved Standard DAS profile and named shared-security/assurance services.'
   - Recommendation: In the pack, DAS = Bounded Distributed Autonomous System (vocabulary.md:1345); in every blockchain reader's vocabulary, DAS = Data Availability Sampling (Celestia, Ethereum danksharding). An L1 specification is the one document guaranteed to be read by people who will parse 'Standard DAS profile' and 'StandardDASProfileRegistry' as a data-availability conformance standard — and neither L1 file ever expands the acronym. Rename in the L1-facing docs to 'Standard Autonomous-System Profile' (StandardASProfileRegistry) or spell out 'Standard (Bounded) DAS — Distributed Autonomous System' at first use in both files; the registry name should change before any interface is implemented, since contract names ossify.
15. **Dangling 'HHAI' acronym and duplicated/orphaned contract names in the mainnet family lists** — `low` · Refactor
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ioi-l1-mainnet.md:386 — '## HHAI Governance Duties' (acronym appears nowhere else in the pack); `RoutingDecisionRoot` listed at both :217 and :238
   - Recommendation: Three cleanups in ioi-l1-mainnet.md: (1) 'HHAI' is defined nowhere in either file or vocabulary.md — rename the heading 'IOI Network Governance Duties' or add HHAI to vocabulary.md; an unexpandable acronym in a canonical governance section damages credibility. (2) RoutingDecisionRoot appears as a distinct contract in two families — dedupe to the single RoutingDecisionRootRegistry the interfaces file defines. (3) UsageSettlement (:231) has no interface and its name implies per-usage on-chain settlement, contradicting 'The chain prices commitments. The runtime prices work.' (:318) — rename to AggregatedUsageRoot or fold into SettlementIntentRegistry.


## Part III.15 — Economics and assurance

### Economic flywheel, pricing boundaries, Work Credits, marketplace neutrality

**Files reviewed:** `foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md (1020)` · `domains/marketplace-neutrality.md (316)`

**Current shape.** The flywheel doc declares a stack-wide monetization doctrine: product surfaces (Hypervisor, ioi.ai, aiagent.xyz, sas.xyz, wallet.network) monetize, while substrate (Agentgres writes, authority checks, receipts, routine L1 use) stays unpriced. Three explicitly non-automatic value loops are named: L0 product subscriptions, network services/marketplaces, and L1 scarce neutral trust. The commercial shape is one seat-like Goal Space subscription bundling conductor, memory, policy, receipts, and replay, plus a bounded monthly grant of non-transferable Work Credits; heavy work uses top-ups, opt-in overage, or committed-spend drawdown, and independent Network/Open labor must be funded through separate goal budgets, bounties, procurement caps, or service orders. Work Credits normalize heterogeneous model/runtime/connector/verifier costs into one budget unit while receipts preserve full route and cost decomposition, and BYOK/local runs are never percentage-taxed. Commercial activation is gated: the doc admits current metering is a flat 0.1-OCU-per-receipt slice not fit for commercial allowances and sequences paid allowances behind supplier-invoice reconciliation, capped prepaid beta, and bounded cohort COGS. A procurement hierarchy (open weights → provider APIs → dedicated capacity → OEM authorization → named-user seats last) plus a fail-closed ModelRouteRightsContract governs model supply. The marketplace-neutrality doc bans first-party appropriation: routing must be evidence-based and emit challengeable RoutingDecisionReceipts, the disclosed IOI seed fleet gets no hidden preference, and attribution/payout follows a receipt→verification→acceptance→adjudication→settlement ladder under 15 invariants.

**What it already gets right:**

- Commercial activation gates are unusually honest: the doc names its own live metering (flat 0.1 OCU per model-backed receipt) as insufficient for a paid multi-provider allowance and sequences paid allowances behind supplier-invoice reconciliation, cohort contribution margin, and bounded p95 COGS — the discipline Cursor and Replit learned publicly and expensively in 2025.
- The margin thesis explicitly rejects opaque token resale and retail-chat-plan-sum allowance pricing, locating durable margin in orchestration, governance, managed capacity, and distribution — the only places margin can survive model-cost commoditization.
- ModelRouteRightsContract economic admission (fail-closed missing rights, real OpenAI/Anthropic/OpenRouter ToS citations, prohibition on pooled named-user seats as production capacity) is ahead of essentially every shipping agent platform, which typically discovers resale/automation-rights limits via provider enforcement letters.
- Marketplace neutrality goes beyond DMA-style legal obligations by making self-preferencing auditable in the data model: RoutingDecisionReceipts bind candidate sets, affiliations, attempts, and reason codes, and the first-party seed fleet must be disclosed, costed, and replaceable.
- Clean fee-illegitimacy boundaries (no percentage fees on BYO/local spend, no wallet authority tolls, no Agentgres pricing surface) remove the platform-tax adoption killers, and the conformance-question checklist gives future contributors a real rubric against toll-booth drift.

**SOTA verdict: `behind`** — compared against: Metronome / Orb / Stripe Billing: credit ledgers with expiring grants, prioritized drawdown, holds, plan versioning, draft invoices; FinOps FOCUS 1.x normalized multi-provider cost/billing schema (native in AWS/Azure/GCP exports); OpenAI/Anthropic/Cursor AI credit and allowance pricing lessons, including Cursor's June-2025 repricing trust damage from unbounded agent burn; Two-sided marketplace cold-start economics (atomic networks, hard-side seeding, exploration traffic) and a16z take-rate design; DMA Article 6 self-preferencing obligations and app-store neutrality practice; OpenRouter aggregator economics, BYOK passthrough, ZDR routing

**Where the state of the art is ahead (gaps):**

- No credit-ledger object model: grants, expiry, holds/reservations, drawdown priority, negative-balance and refund semantics — table stakes in Metronome/Orb/Stripe (prose names expiry policy, reserved budget, and refund/adjustment, but no canonical objects)
- No rating/mediation pipeline or FOCUS-aligned normalized cost schema behind the 'invoice-grade reconciliation' requirement; billed categories are prose lists
- No quote/hold object with an overrun policy, despite 'quotes and caps' and 'fallback cost amplification' being named repeatedly
- No rate-card versioning governance (notice, grandfathering, quote-version binding) beyond a named price-schedule-version reconciliation field — the exact failure mode of 2025 AI-credit repricings
- Take-rate design incomplete: trust-value legitimacy conditions exist, but no bands or anti-disintermediation analysis, while the open-L0 covenant increases leakage pressure
- No demand-side cold-start mechanism: seed fleet is supply-only; no atomic-network wedge, demand subsidy, or exploration-traffic policy
- No settlement-side fraud gating (funds age, chargeback clawback windows, payer-payee sybil linkage) between RewardClaim and payout

**Findings (16, severity-ordered):**

1. **The flywheel has no demand-origination engine — all three loops describe supply-side value capture** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:27 — 'IOI has three distinct value loops. They may reinforce one another, but none accrues to another automatically'
   - Recommendation: The doc rigorously explains why nothing accrues automatically, and it correctly gates token/L1 mechanics behind demonstrated demand (:875-885), but it never says where buyer demand originates: take-rates, escrow fees, and L1 settlement all price a transaction flow nothing in the stack generates, and the only cold-start plan is the supply-side seed fleet (:727-732, 'anchor liquidity'). Add a demand-origination section: name the first atomic network (one vertical outcome category on sas.xyz with IOI-funded escrowed bounties or first-party procurement, disclosed under the same neutrality rules as the seed fleet), and state which loop funds the others in years 1-2. Two-sided marketplace doctrine (Cold Start Problem) requires seeding the hard side AND constraining to an atomic market; the canon does the honesty half but not the mechanism half. Downgraded from critical: the activation gates and token sequencing mean the program will not ship broken economics — the gap is a missing plan to create demand, not a defect that ships.
2. **Work Credits are load-bearing across five surfaces but have no credit-ledger object model** — `high` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:377 — '`WorkCredit` is the broad product usage and budget abstraction for managed autonomous work across Hypervisor, ioi.ai, aiagent.xyz, sas.xyz'
   - Recommendation: Define canonical ledger objects: WorkCreditGrant (source, expiry, plan ref), WorkCreditHold (quote-bound reservation), WorkCreditDebit (receipt-reconciled), WorkCreditAdjustment (refund/goodwill), with drawdown priority (expiring grants → included → top-up → committed drawdown) and negative-balance policy, owned by identity-access-and-metering.md or a new work-credits doc. The semantics already exist scattered in prose — grant expiry/reset by plan policy (:382-383), 'estimated cost, reserved budget, finalized supplier cost … refund/adjustment' in the activation-gate data list (:539-540), and 'Work Credit ledgers' named in conformance question 11 (:983) — but no doc owns the objects. Metronome, Orb, and Stripe Billing treat expiring grants, prioritized drawdown, and holds as table-stakes primitives; a five-surface credit economy with only prose semantics will be reinvented inconsistently per surface — the drift engine this canon exists to prevent. This stays consistent with README Non-Negotiable 18 (bounded, non-transferable product budget).
3. **No WorkQuote/hold object binds estimate → reservation → final cost, so 'fallback cost amplification' has no mechanism** — `high` · Better way
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:441 — 'Quotes and caps should be available before expensive or externally procured work.'
   - Recommendation: Define a canonical WorkQuote {scope ref, cost breakdown by billed category, cap, validity window, rate-card version ref, overrun_policy: fail_closed | consent_required | absorb} plus a WorkCreditHold bound to it; require quote+hold before externally procured, Network/Open, or Compare work, and bind final receipts to the quote ref. The activation gates already demand 'estimated cost, reserved budget, finalized supplier cost' (:539) and name fallback cost amplification as a gate (:556), but nothing bounds who eats overruns — the exact place agent billing blows up. This reuses the pack's proven quote-gated-create pattern from the provider adapters.
4. **'Route savings' is named as durable margin, but the accountable-charge formula passes cascade savings to the customer and no optimization fee basis is defined** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:112 — 'Durable margin comes from the conductor, verified cascade and route savings, governed runtime, memory/context continuity'
   - Recommendation: The Routing Fee Covenant does permit optimization fees — 'Optimized placement: routing, procurement, comparison, failover, or aggregation fee' (:684-688) and legitimate revenue surface 9 (:581-583) explicitly cover verified route selection — so the original claim that the covenant 'gives that value away' was overstated. The real gap: under the doc's own cost-plus accountable charge (:421-427, supplier cost + declared IOI charge), cheap-first cascade savings flow through to the customer, so 'route savings' is customer value, not IOI margin, unless a fee basis is declared. Either define one — e.g., a receipt-bound, capped share-of-verified-savings versus the pinned baseline, disclosed in the quote alongside the existing 'IOI fee basis and amount' receipt field (:432) — or delete 'route savings' from the margin thesis and state plainly that margin = subscription + managed-infra + marketplace operations.
5. **Work Credits may budget sas.xyz service execution while payouts settle in cash — the denomination and conversion boundary is underspecified** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:404 — 'sas service execution, verifier work, delivery bundles, and dispute evidence processing' vs :955 — 'ordinary seat credits silently funding independent Network/Open labor'
   - Recommendation: This is an underspecification, not a strict contradiction: the doc consistently separates buyer-side credit budgeting from settlement rails (:407-410) and forbids credit→cash conversion (marketplace-neutrality.md:229-231). What is missing is the conversion boundary itself. State the denomination rule explicitly: whether Network/Open budgets (:303-306, :340-342) are credit-denominated on the buyer side (separate grant, explicit consent) while payouts settle in fiat/stable/IOI, and define who fixes the credit→cash rate, when it locks (at quote/escrow), and who carries FX/spread risk when a credit-funded bounty pays a fiat-settled worker. Today 'separately funded' (:105) is ambiguous between separate money and a separate budget envelope, and escrow denomination for sas.xyz orders is nowhere specified.
6. **Take-rate design lacks bands and an anti-disintermediation doctrine while the open-L0 covenant increases leakage pressure** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:767 — 'marketplace take-rate on paid worker usage, installs, subscriptions, procurement, or managed instance revenue'
   - Recommendation: The doc already sketches the right principle: aiagent.xyz take-rate applies 'only where it supplies real demand, trust, hosting, or settlement' (:259) and the anti-pattern list bans 'sas.xyz take-rate without escrow, SLA, procurement, billing, dispute, delivery, or commercial trust value' (:944-945). But it never confronts post-discovery leakage: the covenant exempts a self-hosted user invoking their own worker (:857-858) and bans fees on runs without marketplace distribution (:938-940), while the open substrate lets a buyer who discovered a worker on aiagent.xyz deal with its publisher directly off-platform, fee-free. Add an anti-disintermediation doctrine: take-rate attaches only to non-leakable assurance benefits — on-platform verified work history, escrow/dispute rights, reputation accrual, managed-instance SLAs — and declare expected take-rate bands per value tier (payment-only ~3-5%; escrow+SLA+dispute ~10-20%) so the two marketplaces don't improvise. Note the original evidence reading of :856 was imprecise (the exemption covers a user's own worker, not marketplace-discovered workers).
7. **Neutrality invariants leave production-grade evidence hard to earn for entrants; add a receipted exploration-traffic policy** — `medium` · Better way *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:780 — 'routing, reputation, and managed-instance trust must be earned through receipts, evals, and successful work'
   - Recommendation: The ratchet is softer than a pure chicken-and-egg: paid benchmark/certification execution (:763-764), drafts/unverified listings (:778-779), and 'benchmark performance' as a declared routing basis (marketplace-neutrality.md:96) give entrants an evidence ramp without production traffic. But production work history and calibration-grade reputation still require real routed work, which the router rationally allocates to proven workers — structurally favoring the disclosed first-party seed fleet. Canonize an exploration policy: a declared fraction of Auto/1-of-N traffic allocated to benchmarked-but-unproven workers, restricted to low-risk task classes with spend caps, emitted as RoutingDecisionReceipts with reason `exploration`, funded as market operations. This satisfies (not violates) neutrality invariant 6 — a declared policy basis with a challengeable receipt trail. Ad marketplaces and Amazon-style new-seller boosts treat exploration slates as core neutrality machinery.
8. **The Verified Work Graph moat requires cross-party evidence aggregation that the sovereignty doctrine disincentivizes** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:155 — 'cross-party Verified Work Graph aggregation' vs docs/architecture/domains/marketplace-neutrality.md:256 — 'They may sell bounded work, evidence, access, or rights while retaining private cognition, data, memory'
   - Recommendation: Routing calibration, reputation, and marketplace trust (:241-251) — the network layer's core paid products — need calibration-grade evidence from many sovereign domains, but conditional-cooperation doctrine (INV-30) says parties rationally share the minimum. The neutrality doc already names the consideration classes (payment, licensing, reciprocal access, portable reputation, reusable learning — marketplace-neutrality.md:253-255), so the raw incentive vocabulary exists; what is missing is the priced bargain. Define it in the enrollment table: what an ioi_connected domain receives for permitting aggregation (routing-quality dividends, assurance discounts, portable reputation) as explicit service terms. Otherwise the 'durable compounding asset' claim (:40) risks the classic data-moat fallacy, and the doc's own honesty (:45-46 'no moat by itself') concedes the input never arrives.
9. **wallet.network revenue concentrates on regulated value movement with only policy-hook-level compliance doctrine and no regulated-entity posture** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:807 — 'trades, prediction/event-market exposure, and advanced financial actions'
   - Recommendation: The original 'zero regulatory-perimeter doctrine' claim was too strong: the linked owner doc (components/wallet-network/product-exchange-risk.md) models compliance as policy modules (blocked jurisdictions, sanctions screening, travel-rule-style metadata, :1214-1230), requires explicit disclosure when Wallet charges a fee or spread (:1156-1168), and excludes venue execution, liquidity, and venue-native positions as non-goals — venues execute (:75-93, :536-538) — a de-facto venue-of-record posture. What remains missing is the regulated-entity question: when wallet.network itself charges fees or spreads on swaps, payments, or settlement assistance, who is the money transmitter / VASP of record? Add a regulatory-perimeter note per revenue line: partner-of-record / licensed-sponsor model (the Stripe/Bridge pattern), jurisdiction-scoped enablement, or explicit deferral, covering MSB/VASP, MiCA, and CFTC event-contract regimes. The flywheel doc gives model-provider ToS four cited paragraphs (:508-518); its designated wallet revenue zone deserves the same treatment.
10. **1020-line monolith owns ~10 areas and duplicates the ModelRouteRightsContract field list it forbids duplicating** — `medium` · Refactor *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:494 — 'pricing code must not invent a reduced parallel contract'
   - Recommendation: Lines 485-494 enumerate 25+ contract fields whose schema is owned by model-router doctrine — a prose parallel contract that will drift on the next schema change, ironically beside the sentence banning exactly that. Split the file: (a) keep pricing-boundaries doctrine (thesis, legitimacy surfaces, anti-patterns, conformance questions); (b) extract Work Credits + Commercial Activation Gates into a billing-mechanics owner doc alongside the ledger/quote objects; (c) move Open Supply And Foundation-Model Procurement into model-router doctrine, leaving one economic-admission sentence plus a link. The header's ten-topic ownership claim (:4-8) is itself the drift signal.
11. **Invoice-grade multi-provider reconciliation has no normalized cost schema; adopt FinOps FOCUS** — `medium` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:538 — 'uncached input, cache write/read, visible output, reasoning, image, audio, tool, accelerator, environment, storage, and other billed categories'
   - Recommendation: Adopt FOCUS 1.x as the canonical supplier-cost projection schema in Agentgres — BilledCost/EffectiveCost/PricingUnit/SkuPriceId/ChargeCategory — extended with route-attempt and receipt refs, and map provider invoices plus aggregator statements into it. The reconciliation requirement (:534-542) is currently a prose wish-list of categories; FOCUS is the existing industry answer (native in AWS/Azure/GCP billing exports since 2024-25) and turns 'reconciled to supplier statements' from bespoke per-provider parsing into testable schema conformance — the same move the pack already makes with conformance suites elsewhere.
12. **No rate-card/plan versioning governance or repricing mechanics despite receipts referencing a 'price schedule'** — `medium` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:430 — 'the receipt ledger preserves at least the actual Worker composition, model/provider/endpoint and route, price schedule'
   - Recommendation: The doc already requires 'price-schedule version' as a reconciliation datum (:535), so version identity exists in prose; what is missing is the governance around it. Add RateCardVersion as a first-class object (effective window, per-category credit rates, receipt-bindable ref) with rules: work admitted under a quote bills at the quoted version; mid-cycle repricing requires notice plus existing-grant protection; committed-spend true-ups reference the contracted version. Stripe Billing and Metronome treat plan versioning and migration as first-class. The doc absorbs the allowance-sizing lesson from 2025 AI pricing failures but not the repricing-under-fire lesson (Cursor June 2025); when provider costs shift, the credit-to-dollar rate must move through a governed, receipt-visible object, not silent rate-card edits.
13. **Settlement-side fraud loop (stolen-card credits → sybil worker → fiat payout) has visibility but no gate** — `medium` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:556 — 'contribution margin, bounded p95 COGS, abuse and chargeback behavior' and docs/architecture/domains/marketplace-neutrality.md:133 — 'collusion signals must remain visible to routing'
   - Recommendation: Neutrality rule 12 makes sybil/collusion signals visible to settlement policy, and the assurance ladder gates payouts on verification/acceptance state (marketplace-neutrality.md:225-227), but the contribution-object list (:141-160) defines RewardClaim with no payments-risk object gating it — assurance state is a quality gate, not a fraud gate. Add SettlementRiskPolicy between RewardClaim and payout: funds-age minimums, payer↔payee sybil-linkage checks, clawback windows aligned to card chargeback periods, and velocity caps on fresh accounts. The credit→marketplace→payout chain this facet defines (via separately funded Network/Open budgets) is precisely the laundering primitive every credit marketplace attracts; 'abuse and chargeback behavior' as a beta progression gate is an observation plan, not a mechanism.
14. **'Work Credits' reads as compensation earned for work, forcing repeated cross-doc disclaimers** — `low` · Rename *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:381 — 'Work Credits are non-transferable product credits, not cash, a speculative token, a worker payout rail'
   - Recommendation: Rename to `UsageCredit` or 'Managed Work Allowance'. Every other 'Work' noun in this economy (WorkResult, Verified Work Graph, worker payouts, ContributionReceipt) denotes labor performed, so 'Work Credit' invites the exact worker-side misreading the canon must repeatedly disclaim (:381, :958 anti-pattern, marketplace-neutrality.md:229-231, and README Non-Negotiable 18). A demand-side name eliminates the earner confusion, the transferability temptation, and the support burden of explaining that the unit buyers see next to worker payouts is not what workers are paid in. Note: the term is pinned in README Non-Negotiable 18 and used pack-wide, so this is a coordinated rename across the canon, cheapest now while Work Credits remain 'planned'; the semantics of NN-18 are unchanged either way.
15. **Compare/N-of-N is economically dominated: maximum cost with silently minimized output rights** — `low` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:496 — 'Compare or synthesized multi-route output uses the intersection of every contributing route's customer output rights'
   - Recommendation: Compare bills all admitted attempts plus verifier and synthesis work (:440-441) yet yields only the rights intersection, so one restrictive route silently downgrades the most expensive mode's output usability — a trap discovered after paying. Require the Compare quote to disclose the computed rights intersection before spend, and support a rights-floor constraint in route policy ('exclude routes granting less than X') so the router prunes rights-degrading candidates. This keeps the legally correct intersection rule while making its economic consequence visible at decision time.
16. **Two credit-like units (live OCU vs planned WorkCredit) with no declared migration or grade relationship** — `low` · Rename *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md:524 — 'the live OCU slice charges `0.1` OCU for every model-backed receipt'
   - Recommendation: OCU appears in this doc unexpanded (:14, :524) and in receipts today, while Work Credits become the commercial unit tomorrow. The doc already flags the flat per-receipt constant as commercially inadequate (conformance question 15, :993-994) and sequences its replacement through the activation gates, but never states the unit relationship. Either declare OCU an internal telemetry unit never rendered in commercial UX (and expand the acronym on first use here), or collapse to one unit — WorkCredit with a grade field (estimate | reconciled | invoiced) — so coarse metering and commercial billing are one ledger at different assurance grades, mirroring the pack's own attestation→verification ladder. Dual units with no conversion doctrine is a reconciliation and user-trust drift seed at exactly the boundary the activation gates police.

### Ecosystem assurance, certification, liability

**Files reviewed:** `foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md (974)`

**Current shape.** The file defines Ecosystem Assurance as a source-neutral trust layer that declares — but never enforces — trust postures over IOI subjects (workers, runtimes, wallet clients, MCP gateways, embodied deployments, services, OutcomeRooms). Its object families are: EcosystemAssuranceProfile (what must be proven), ConformanceProfile (interface/behavior compatibility with negative tests), CertificationClaim (subject-profile-evidence-issuer binding with expiry and revocation), JurisdictionPolicyPack (machine-readable compliance constraints compiled into wallet/daemon/Agentgres gates), AssuranceEvidenceBundle, AssurancePostureProjection (rebuildable operator/customer posture view), ComplianceAuditExportBundle (redaction-manifest audit exports), LiabilityClaimRoute (claims routing over evidence, explicitly never coverage adjudication), EcosystemAbuseSignal/QuarantineAdvisory (scoped receipted abuse response), and CommercialAssuranceExport (billing/SLA/tax exports). Assurance strength follows a progressive ladder (attested → evidenced → verified → accepted → adjudicated → settled, defined pack-wide in vocabulary.md and INV-21) that must never be collapsed in UI or economics, and the top network tier ioi_secured requires a current "Standard DAS" profile plus named security services and bonds. Enforcement always stays with the owning plane — wallet.network revokes authority, the daemon blocks invocation, marketplaces delist, Agentgres records truth — and IOI L1 anchors only commitments explicitly selected by enrollment and settlement profiles. Doctrine status is canonical; implementation status is speculative (design-only).

**What it already gets right:**

- The non-collapsing progressive assurance ladder plus honest scope disclaimers (a passing result 'proves conformance only to the named version and evidence window', lines 512-515; embodied results bound to exact hardware/firmware baselines, 639-643) is more honest than industry 'verified' badges and matches SLSA's provenance-is-not-security discipline.
- Enforcement-stays-with-owner routing (lines 825-834) structurally prevents the layer from becoming a shadow authority plane or GRC kill switch — the classic failure mode of enterprise compliance tooling — and is consistent with INV-1/INV-27.
- ComplianceAuditExportBundle's export manifest with explicit included/redacted/protected/excluded refs and typed exclusion_reasons (lines 432-443, 470-475) is ahead of prevailing SOC 2 evidence-dump practice and close to what regulators are pushing toward.
- QuarantineAdvisory carrying dependent_refs, blast_radius_report_ref, appeal_policy_ref, and release_conditions (lines 807-820) is more principled than typical opaque marketplace delisting or app-store takedowns.
- Modeling insurance as 'claim routing over evidence, not an assurance layer pretending to decide coverage' (lines 711-712) is the right humility — most schemes overclaim here.

**SOTA verdict: `behind`** — compared against: SLSA v1.x levels + Verification Summary Attestations; in-toto attestations / DSSE signed envelopes; C2PA 2.x conformance program and signing-credential trust lists; Certificate Transparency / Sigstore Rekor transparency logs; OCSP/CRL revocation distribution; EU AI Act conformity assessment (Annex VI internal control vs Annex VII notified body; Art. 73 serious-incident reporting deadlines); ISO/IEC 17065/17011 accreditation of certification bodies; UL/CE notified-body mark economics; SOC 2 Type II / ISO/IEC 42001 auditor ecosystem; NIST OSCAL machine-readable control and assessment language; FedRAMP continuous monitoring and significant-change recertification; AI insurance underwriting practice 2025-2026 (Munich Re aiSure, Armilla/Lloyd's AI performance coverage); GSN assurance cases (partially adopted via EmbodiedDeploymentAssuranceCase)

**Where the state of the art is ahead (gaps):**

- No machine-evaluable requirement or attestation format: profile requirements are prose strings and CertificationClaim has no signature envelope, unlike OSCAL, in-toto/DSSE, and SLSA VSA (the pack has a signed-envelope convention in common-objects-and-envelopes.md, but this file does not adopt it)
- No issuer accreditation object, independence requirement, or certifier liability chain — the load-bearing economics of ISO 17065/17011, notified bodies, and UL marks
- No trust roots, transparency log, or revocation distribution protocol for claims consumed across domains (C2PA trust lists, CT/Rekor, OCSP)
- No mapping of profile families to real conformity regimes or deadline-bound regulator incident reporting (EU AI Act Art. 73)
- No object binding insurance coverage to profile versions and telemetry obligations, though 2025-2026 AI underwriting prices exactly on that
- No tiered assurance depth within a profile family for staged adoption (SLSA 1-4, CC EALs, FedRAMP baselines) — the ioi_compatible/ioi_connected/ioi_secured enrollment ladder stages what a system may claim, but standard_das itself is one maximal gate
- No delta-recertification / continuous-monitoring cost model (FedRAMP ConMon), so refresh-on-any-change is economically undefined

**Findings (13, severity-ordered):**

1. **IOI is structurally the liable certifier of last resort for its own top trust tier** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:484 — "`standard_das` is the IOI Network conformance/assurance family for a bounded distributed autonomous system."
   - Recommendation: IOI authors the profile, gates ioi_secured on it (line 525), and names no independent issuer class for the Standard DAS CertificationClaim itself — so when a certified system fails, the wrongful certification lands reputationally and legally on IOI, yet LiabilityClaimRoute.subject_refs (730-735) cannot name a certification claim or issuer as a routable subject. Partial mitigation already exists: economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md gives ioi_secured shared-security providers explicit bonds/stake and slashing/claim terms and bonds verifier/guardian/availability-witness/relayer/arbitrator roles — but certifiers/issuers are absent from that bonded-role list, and nothing separates profile authorship from claim issuance. Separate them: IOI governance versions standard_das; accredited third parties issue claims and post slashable bonds via the existing L1 bond anchors (lines 941-942); state explicitly whether IOI-the-org issues CertificationClaims; and add certification_claim:// and issuer org: to LiabilityClaimRoute.subject_refs so negligent certification is itself a routable incident.
2. **No issuer accreditation or certifier-liability economics (ISO 17065/17011, notified bodies, UL mark model)** — `high` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:167 — "issuer_ref: org:... | domain:... | governance_ref:..."
   - Recommendation: Anyone can be an issuer; nothing accredits issuers, requires independence from the subject, or makes issuers carry E&O exposure — the parts of certification economics that make UL/CE marks trustworthy. Add an AssuranceIssuerAccreditation object: accreditor_ref, permitted profile_types/subject_kinds, independence rule (issuer must not be the subject's operator for third-party mode), E&O/bond refs, audit cadence, revocation. Add assessment_mode: self_declared | second_party | third_party_accredited to CertificationClaim and let profiles require a minimum mode — embodied_runtime and ioi_secured should require third_party_accredited, mirroring EU AI Act Annex VII vs Annex VI. economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md covers fee bases and bonds verifier/guardian/availability-witness/relayer/arbitrator roles, but certifiers/issuers appear nowhere in its bonded-role list and nothing accredits them.
3. **Profile requirements are prose strings — the loop's 'measure' step cannot be automated and every issuer will interpret differently** — `high` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:175 — "- receipt_type: string" (and :176 "requirement: string", :211 "- condition: string")
   - Recommendation: For a layer whose stated purpose is machine-legible institutional trust, required_evidence.requirement and negative_tests.condition being freeform prose means 'measure evidence against profile requirements' (line 86) is human judgment, conformance results are not comparable across issuers, and the drift this file claims to supersede is recreated inside its core object. Define a typed requirement-expression grammar bound to canonical-enums receipt types and event names (predicates over receipt fields, freshness windows, counts, verifier identity), or adopt NIST OSCAL component-definition/assessment-plan shapes. Negative tests should reference executable eval worlds via eval_world_ref — Foundry already owns 'executable eval worlds' (lines 124-126) — instead of condition strings.
4. **Refresh-on-any-change certification with no delta-recertification semantics yields perpetually stale or rubber-stamp certificates** — `high` · Red flag
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:246 — "Claims should expire or refresh when the subject, profile, dependency, jurisdiction pack, runtime posture, evidence base, or threat model changes."
   - Recommendation: Agent packages change model routes, tools, and dependencies weekly; full re-certification per change is economically impossible, so 'should refresh' becomes an ignored norm — the one-time-badge anti-pattern (963-964) through the back door, with the unpaid cost silently landing on subject owners. Add per-profile recertification_triggers: change-class → required evidence subset (model-route change re-runs eval scorecards only; authority-scope change re-runs blast-radius evidence; jurisdiction-pack change re-runs policy-decision receipts). This is FedRAMP continuous-monitoring/significant-change analysis plus SLSA's core insight: certify the subject's change-admission process rather than every artifact, and let receipts carry per-version deltas under the certified process.
5. **liability_status 'covered' has no owning fact and violates the file's own anti-pattern** — `medium` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:371 — "none | covered | claim_open | disputed | uncovered | unknown" vs :958 — "an insurance coverage adjudicator;"
   - Recommendation: Projections are rebuildable from Agentgres operations, receipts, policy packs, wallet refs, marketplace and service-order state (379-380) — none of which is defined to record insurance policy state, so 'covered'/'uncovered' is either uncomputable or an implicit coverage adjudication the anti-patterns forbid. Add an InsuranceCoverageBinding object (external policy ref, insurer ref, covered profile_refs plus versions, insurer-required telemetry/evidence obligations, exclusions, effective window, lapse conditions) admitted like any other evidence ref, and derive liability_status only from it — this mirrors how Munich Re aiSure and Lloyd's/Armilla policies bind coverage to named system versions. Also split the collapsed axes: coverage_status (none/bound/lapsed/unknown) vs claim_status (none/open/disputed/settled).
6. **CertificationClaim has no signature, envelope, or trust-root mechanism — relying parties cannot verify claims** — `medium` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:242 — "public_anchor_ref: commitment://... | settlement://... | tx://... | null"
   - Recommendation: The only cryptographic binding on the claim object is an optional public anchor; a relying party in another domain (aiagent.xyz consuming worker posture, line 548) cannot verify who issued a claim or that it was not altered. The pack already has the needed convention — common-objects-and-envelopes.md gives ManifestEnvelope and AIIP envelopes a signature block (scheme/public_key_ref/signature), and INV-28 makes cross-domain handoffs signed at the transport layer — but transport-level signing does not substitute for claim-level issuer verification once a claim is stored, re-served, or consumed outside a live handoff. Adopt that envelope (in-toto/DSSE framing: subject digest + predicate type + issuer signature) for CertificationClaim and conformance results, add issuance_authority_ref: authority://... so issuing a claim is itself a wallet-authorized, receipted act (the CertificationClaimReceipt at 918 exists but the claim object binds no issuing authority), and define issuer key discovery via wallet.network-owned trust lists, following C2PA's certified-signing-credential model. All of this stays within local-first, sparse-settlement physics.
7. **standard_das is a monolithic mega-profile; certification at this bar will have zero early issuers or subjects** — `medium` · Red flag *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:505 — "migration, succession/adoption, authority rotation, dissolution, decommission, data/state/evidence export, residual-asset, obligation, and network-exit semantics;"
   - Recommendation: The single profile (485-508) requires constitution enforcement, failover/fencing/RPO-RTO evidence, oracle profiles, succession and dissolution semantics, and negative self-propagation tests — roughly SOC 2 Type II plus a high-EAL Common Criteria evaluation in one gate; CC's history shows that cost/duration profile kills adoption, which is why CC collapsed into cPP-based evaluations. The enrollment ladder (ioi_compatible/ioi_connected/ioi_secured, lines 521-525) and the honest-PoA-1 allowance (510-512) stage what a system may claim, but they do not stage assurance depth: any system wanting ioi_secured faces the full bar at once. Tier it as standard_das levels 1-4 (level 1: declared constitution, typed receipts, fail-closed negative tests; level 4: full lifecycle/succession/exit evidence), the way SLSA 1-4 and FedRAMP Low/Moderate/High stage assurance, and let ioi_secured require a named minimum tier per service class.
8. **'ecosystem'-scope quarantine with untyped restrictions and nullable appeal contradicts the no-global-kill-switch rule** — `medium` · Contradiction
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:755 — "It must not become an unbounded global kill switch." vs :804 — "embodied_domain | ecosystem"
   - Recommendation: restrictions is '- string' (805-806) and appeal_policy_ref is nullable (817). Enforcement staying with owners (825-834) means an advisory is not literally a kill switch — owners decide what to do with it — but if owners compile advisories into gates as intended, an ecosystem-scoped advisory carrying arbitrary prose restrictions with no appeal and no named authoring owner is functionally close to one, and a defamation/lost-business liability magnet for whoever issues it (the file never names who may author ecosystem-scope advisories). Require for ecosystem scope: issuer must be a governance_ref, appeal_policy_ref non-null, restrictions drawn from a typed enum aligned with recommended_action (789-791), a mandatory expiry/re-affirmation window, and mandatory public anchoring for accountability. Name the advisory-issuing function's owner explicitly in Ownership Boundaries (104-139).
9. **No EU AI Act conformity route or deadline-bound regulator incident reporting** — `medium` · SOTA gap *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:700 — "sector-specific review rules for finance, health, insurance, employment, physical action, critical infrastructure, education, legal, and public-sector workflows;"
   - Recommendation: By mid-2026 the EU AI Act high-risk regime — conformity assessment (internal control vs notified body), CE marking, post-market monitoring, and Art. 73 serious-incident reporting with fixed deadlines — is the concrete regime an embodied_runtime or service_outcome deployment in the EU must satisfy, and the doc's generic sector list cannot express it. Add incident_reporting_rules to JurisdictionPolicyPack (regulator_ref, trigger classes, deadline, required report shape), add a regulator_report route from EcosystemAbuseSignal/LiabilityClaimRoute with deadline tracking surfaced in AssurancePostureProjection, and map profile families to conformity-assessment routes so a certification claim can state which regime it supports.
10. **No revocation distribution or transparency log for claims consumed across domains** — `medium` · SOTA gap
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:183 — "public_anchor_policy: never | on_public_listing | on_dispute | on_bond | always"
   - Recommendation: A claim anchored 'never' is auditable only inside the issuer's own Agentgres domain; a cross-domain relying party cannot detect a backdated, swapped, or silently revoked claim, and revocation_policy_ref (182) and revocation_receipt_ref (243) name policy and a local receipt with no distribution mechanism. Add (a) an issuer-operated append-only claim log with inclusion proofs (Certificate Transparency / Sigstore Rekor model) required for any claim consumed cross-domain, and (b) a signed revocation feed or status endpoint that consumers must check within a declared freshness window (OCSP-style), recorded via evidence_watermark in AssurancePostureProjection. L1 anchoring stays the optional stronger tier, preserving sparse-settlement doctrine.
11. **'claim' means both certification assertion and liability demand in the same file** — `medium` · Rename *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:909 — "assurance.claim.opened" (vs :894 "assurance.certification.claimed")
   - Recommendation: The events assurance.claim.opened/routed/closed presumably track LiabilityClaimRoute lifecycle, but CertificationClaim also 'opens', and line 894's assurance.certification.claimed compounds the ambiguity — in insurance context a claim is a compensation demand; in attestation context it is an assertion. Rename the events to assurance.liability_claim.opened|routed|closed, and rename CertificationClaim to CertificationAttestation (matching in-toto/C2PA vocabulary where an attestation is a signed assertion), reserving 'claim' exclusively for the liability/dispute sense used by LiabilityClaimRoute and 'bonds/claims' (525). Update vocabulary.md:121 and the receipt family names (918-919) in the same pass.
12. **ConformanceResult is load-bearing but undefined — define a Verification-Summary-style object** — `medium` · Better way
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:235 — "- conformance_result://..."
   - Recommendation: CertificationClaim binds conformance_result_refs and the receipt families include ConformanceRunReceipt (917), but no owner defines the result object (the conformance_result:// scheme appears exactly once in the pack): who executed the suite, under which harness/runtime/environment, against which profile version, which negative tests ran with what outcomes, over what evidence window. SLSA's Verification Summary Attestation is the exact SOTA shape: subject digest, policy/profile ref plus version, verifier identity, verification time, result, dependency levels. Define ConformanceResult here or in common-objects-and-envelopes.md with those fields plus eval_world_ref and Foundry job refs, so aiagent.xyz ranking and ioi_secured enrollment consume typed, comparable results rather than opaque refs.
13. **Commercial assurance exports belong with the economics owner, not the certification file** — `low` · Refactor *(confidence: medium)*
   - Evidence: docs/architecture/foundations/ecosystem-assurance-certification-liability.md:6 — "quarantine posture, liability and claims routing, and commercial assurance exports."
   - Recommendation: CommercialAssuranceExport (852-877) is billing/invoice/SLA/tax export shape — the subject matter of economic-flywheel-and-pricing-boundaries.md, which already owns invoices and fee bases. The header's consolidation is deliberate (it supersedes 'plan prose that scatters ... billing assurance ... across ... docs'), but the result is one foundations file owning six loosely coupled domains (certification, conformance, jurisdiction, insurance, abuse, billing), diluting the certification core it should be deepening. Move CommercialAssuranceExport and the Commercial Assurance section (836-882) to the economics owner, leaving only the evidence hooks (evidence_bundle_refs, audit_export_refs) here. Keep abuse/quarantine co-located for now since compatibility_level: revoked (214) couples it to certification lifecycle.

## Appendix B — Method detail, statistics, and this review's own gaps

### Method

- **Corpus:** every live file under `docs/architecture` — 82 markdown files, ~75,800 lines. `_archive/` was excluded as superseded; `whitepaper.tex` was out of scope (it is a publishing synthesis, not an owner).
- **Facet pass:** 30 reviewers, each assigned a facet (2–7 files, or a half of `common-objects-and-envelopes.md`, which at 9,375 lines was split with a shared heading map). Each was required to read its files completely, compare against named mid-2026 state-of-the-art anchors it is confident exist, respect the pack's own physics (fail-closed authority, receipts, local-first truth, projections-never-truth), and treat "not built yet" as a non-finding while treating canon overreach relative to buildability as a finding.
- **Adversarial verification pass:** an independent verifier per facet re-read every cited region (±80 lines), killed findings whose evidence did not exist or whose criticism the doc already addresses, amended inaccurate citations and unfair wording, checked suggestions against the README Non-Negotiables and `foundations/invariants.md`, and re-scored severity conservatively. Verifiers could not add findings.
- **Synthesis pass:** one agent clustered all red-flag/contradiction/critical/high findings into the ranked systemic risks of Part I; another resolved all rename findings against `_meta/vocabulary.md` into the conflict-free naming program of Part II. This front matter and the ten priority actions were composed from those verified outputs.

### Statistics

| Measure | Value |
| --- | --- |
| Facets reviewed | 30 (15 groups) |
| Findings surviving verification | 446 |
| — kept verbatim / amended / killed | 153 / 293 / 4 |
| By kind | red-flag 108 · sota-gap 107 · contradiction 72 · refactor 57 · rename 52 · better-way 50 |
| By severity (verifier-scored) | critical 1 · high 111 · medium 268 · low 66 |
| SOTA verdicts | near-sota 12 · divergent-by-design 6 · behind 12 · sota 0 |
| Systemic risks (Part I) | 12 (3 critical, 7 high, 2 medium) |
| Rename program (Part II) | 3 tiers; ~50 rename findings resolved into one conflict-free table |

Note the severity scales differ by construction: per-finding severity is verifier-scored conservatively (a single `critical`: receipts unverifiable by outside parties), while Part I criticality emerges from clustering many high findings that share one root cause.

### Known gaps of this review

Four additional cross-cutting angles were identified as high-value but not executed (session budget):

1. **trusted-time-and-expiry** — where TTLs, expiries, freshness windows, and receipt timestamps get trustworthy time from, across a stack that distrusts node operators.
2. **cross-plane-failure-coupling** — failure-mode analysis when daemon, Agentgres, authority, and storage planes degrade *together* rather than one at a time.
3. **platform-self-operability** — SRE/observability doctrine for operating IOI itself (the canon governs user work but says little about operating the operator).
4. **canon-schema-evolution** — versioning and migration discipline for the object model itself (partially covered by systemic risk R1 and the meta-layer facets, but not given a dedicated pass).

These are recommended as the first follow-up reviews. Additionally, `wallet-authority` verification and this document's assembly occurred after one review-session interruption; all facet reports on disk were verified complete before synthesis.
